[tool-ebb] exec_command <- Parse gcode, extract object 0 extrusion segments and clusters ($ cat > /tmp/parse_gcode.py <<'PY' import re, math current_obj = None # state: last XYZ position, E value last = None segments = [] # (x1,y1,x2,y2,abs_e1,abs_e2) block_starts = [] # (line, obj, x, y, z, e) with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f, 1): line = line.strip() if not line: continue m = re.match(r'M486\s+S([-\d.]+)', line) if m: s = int(float(m.group(1))) if s >= 0: current_obj = s block_starts.append((idx, s, last, line)) else: current_obj = None continue if line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0'): # parse tokens tok = line.split() com = tok[0] d = {} for t in tok[1:]: if re.match(r'^[XYZEFI]', t): key = t[0] try: d[key] = float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None: x1,y1 = last['X'], last['Y'] x2,y2 = d['X'], d['Y'] e1 = last.get('E', 0) e2 = d.get('E', e1) if current_obj == 0 and e2 > e1: segments.append((x1,y1,x2,y2,e1,e2,idx,com)) last = d elif last is not None: # update only given coords for k in d: last[k] = d[k] PY python3 /tmp/parse_gcode.py && echo parsed)
✓ [approved]
[tool-ebb] exec_command -> parsed
[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 88991 (context_window 128000, estimated input 37985)
Oops the script just defines, doesn't run print stats. Let's modify to compute summary: number segments, bbox, maybe cluster by gaps. Also capture blocks. Let's run.
[tool-e5b] exec_command <- Print stats for object 0 segments and bounding clusters ($ cat > /tmp/parse_gcode2.py <<'PY' import re, math current_obj = None last = None segments = [] blocks = [] with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f, 1): line = line.strip() if not line: continue m = re.match(r'M486\s+S([-\d.]+)', line) if m: s = int(float(m.group(1))) if s >= 0: current_obj = s blocks.append((idx, s, 'start', last)) else: current_obj = None continue if line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0'): tok = line.split() d = {} for t in tok[1:]: if re.match(r'^[XYZEFI]', t): key = t[0] try: d[key] = float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None: x1,y1 = last['X'], last['Y'] x2,y2 = d['X'], d['Y'] e1 = last.get('E', 0) e2 = d.get('E', e1) if current_obj == 0 and e2 > e1: segments.append((idx,x1,y1,x2,y2)) last = d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k] = d[k] print("object-0 extrusion segments:", len(segments)) if segments: xs=[v[1] for v in segments]+[v[3] for v in segments] ys=[v[2] for v in segments]+[v[4] for v in segments] print("bbox x:", min(xs), max(xs), "y:", min(ys), max(ys)) # simple clusters by connected components via coords? just print ranges per line block count print("blocks:", blocks[:20], "... total", len(blocks)) PY python3 /tmp/parse_gcode2.py)

This suggests the "Embossed text" object contains text spread horizontally across x 55 to 204 and vertically y 80-147. Multiple layers lifting Z (emboss). So text might be a phrase in wide coordinates; we can render all object 0 segments in 2D.

Let's generate ASCII art or an image. 18815 segments spanning ~150mm by 67mm. To render glyphs, need decent resolution. Let's create a binary pixel grid using e.g., 0.1mm pixel size → 1500x670 pixels. Could produce ASCII downsampled.

Let's write a rasterizer: for each segment, draw line on grid with given resolution. Use Bresenham or simple sampling. Then downsample/ASCII. Let's also print separate clusters (letters). First produce ASCII image at maybe 200x80.

Let's create the renderer.
[tool-055] exec_command <- Rasterize object 0 extrusion segments and write ASCII art ($ cat > /tmp/render_ascii.py <<'PY' import re, math # --- parse --- current_obj = None last = None segments = [] with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f, 1): line = line.strip() if not line: continue m = re.match(r'M486\s+S([-\d.]+)', line) if m: s = int(float(m.group(1))) current_obj = s if s >= 0 else None continue if line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0'): tok = line.split() d = {} for t in tok[1:]: if re.match(r'^[XYZEFI]', t): key = t[0] try: d[key] = float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None: x1,y1 = last.get('X'), last.get('Y') x2,y2 = d.get('X'), d.get('Y') if x1 is not None and x2 is not None: e1 = last.get('E', 0) e2 = d.get('E', e1) if current_obj == 0 and e2 > e1: segments.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) last = d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k] = d[k] xs=[v[0] for v in segments]+[v[2] for v in segments] ys=[v[1] for v in segments]+[v[3] for v in segments] minx,maxx = min(xs), max(xs) miny,maxy = min(ys), max(ys) print("seg", len(segments), "bbox", minx, maxx, miny, maxy) # Choose grid: aim ~0.1 mm per cell res = 0.09 w = int((maxx-minx)/res)+2 h = int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print("grid", w, h) grid = [[0]*w for _ in range(h)] def raster_seg(x1,y1,x2,y2): # map to pixel coords px1 = int((x1-minx)/res); py1 = int((y1-miny)/res) px2 = int((x2-minx)/res); py2 = int((y2-miny)/res) # Bresenham dx = abs(px2-px1); dy = abs(py2-py1) sx = 1 if px1<px2 else -1 sy = 1 if py1<py2 else -1 err = dx-dy x,y = px1,py1 while True: if 0<=x<w and 0<=y<h: grid[y][x]=1 if x==px2 and y==py2: break e2 = 2*err if e2 > -dy: err -= dy; x += sx if e2 < dx: err += dx; y += sy for s in segments: raster_seg(*s) # Output ASCII, choose aspect: cells ~0.09mm square, char cell aspect ~2:1 (char height ~2x width) # Compress horizontally by 2 to square-ish scale_y = 1 scale_x = 2 out_h = h//scale_y out_w = w//scale_x print("ascii dims", out_w, out_h) chars = " .:-=+*#%@" # density sampling with open('/tmp/ascii.txt','w') as fo: for oy in range(out_h): line_s=[] for ox in range(out_w): count = 0 total=0 for yy in range(oy*scale_y, min((oy+1)*scale_y,h)): row = grid[yy] for xx in range(ox*scale_x, min((ox+1)*scale_x,w)): total+=1 count += row[xx] if count==0: line_s.append(' ') else: # use characters ratio = count/total ch = chars[min(int(ratio*len(chars))//1, len(chars)-1)] line_s.append(ch) fo.write(''.join(line_s).rstrip()+'\n') print("wrote /tmp/ascii.txt") PY python3 /tmp/render_ascii.py && echo "--- ASCII ---" && head -140 /tmp/ascii.txt)

[tool-26b] exec_command <- Render object0 toolpaths to PNG and OCR ($ cat > /tmp/render_png.py <<'PY' import re from PIL import Image, ImageDraw current_obj = None last = None segments = [] with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f, 1): line = line.strip() if not line: continue m = re.match(r'M486\s+S([-\d.]+)', line) if m: s = int(float(m.group(1))) current_obj = s if s >= 0 else None continue if line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0'): tok = line.split() d = {} for t in tok[1:]: if re.match(r'^[XYZEFI]', t): key = t[0] try: d[key] = float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None: x1,y1 = last.get('X'), last.get('Y') x2,y2 = d.get('X'), d.get('Y') if x1 is not None and x2 is not None: e1 = last.get('E', 0) e2 = d.get('E', e1) if current_obj == 0 and e2 > e1: segments.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) last = d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k] = d[k] xs=[v[0] for v in segments]+[v[2] for v in segments] ys=[v[1] for v in segments]+[v[3] for v in segments] minx,maxx,miny,maxy = min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) res = 0.05 # mm per pixel w = int((maxx-minx)/res)+2 h = int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print("image", w, h) img = Image.new('L', (w,h), 255) dr = ImageDraw.Draw(img) for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segments: px1 = int((x1-minx)/res); py1 = int((y1-miny)/res) px2 = int((x2-minx)/res); py2 = int((y2-miny)/res) dr.line([(px1,py1),(px2,py2)], fill=0, width=1) img.save('/tmp/text0.png') print('saved /tmp/text0.png') PY python3 /tmp/render_png.py echo '--- OCR ---' tesseract /tmp/text0.png stdout --psm 6 2>/dev/null)

[tool-593] exec_command <- Extract and render first object0 block ($ cat > /tmp/render_first_block.py <<'PY' import re from PIL import Image, ImageDraw current_obj = None last = None blocks = [] # list of segment lists current_segs = None with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f, 1): line = line.strip() if not line: continue m = re.match(r'M486\s+S([-\d.]+)', line) if m: s = int(float(m.group(1))) if s == 0: current_obj = 0 current_segs = [] blocks.append((idx, current_segs)) elif s == 1: current_obj = 1 current_segs = None else: current_obj = None continue if current_obj == 0: if line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0'): tok = line.split() d = {} for t in tok[1:]: if re.match(r'^[XYZEFI]', t): key = t[0] try: d[key] = float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d and last is not None: x1,y1 = last.get('X'), last.get('Y') x2,y2 = d.get('X'), d.get('Y') if x1 is not None and x2 is not None: e1 = last.get('E', 0) e2 = d.get('E', e1) if e2 > e1: current_segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) last = d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k] = d[k] if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: pass for bi, (sline, segs) in enumerate(blocks[:3]): print("block", bi, "line", sline, "segs", len(segs)) if not segs: continue xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy = min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) res = 0.03 w = int((maxx-minx)/res)+2 h = int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print(" bbox", minx,maxx,miny,maxy, "img", w,h) img = Image.new('L',(w,h),255) dr = ImageDraw.Draw(img) for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: dr.line([(int((x1-minx)/res),int((y1-miny)/res)),(int((x2-minx)/res),int((y2-miny)/res))], fill=0, width=2) img.save(f'/tmp/block{bi}.png') print(" saved /tmp/block{bi}.png") PY python3 /tmp/render_first_block.py echo '--- OCR first block ---' tesseract /tmp/block0.png stdout --psm 6 2>/dev/null)

Wait, E values are in mm of filament, but the moves use small increments *E* relative? Actually in Prusa gcode, `E` is absolute extrusion amount; after `G92 E0` set, E counts filament. But there are E-.7 retractions and E.7 reprime. Then `G1 E.7 F1500` sets absolute E? Hmm, in this file, E values seem to be used as absolute "extrusion length" but small segments have E values like 0.01926, 0.02087, etc., which are cumulative deltas? Let's look:

Line 115: `G1 E-.7 F2100` sets E to -0.7? Then line 120 `G1 E.7 F1500` sets E to 0.7. Then line 125 `G1 X57.481 Y85.701 E.01926`. If E were absolute, 0.01926 is much less than 0.7, not increasing illusionally. So `G1 E.7` may be relative? Wait, G-code convention: `E` on most firmwares (Marlin) is not standard; for Prusa it might represent extrusion *relative to previous*? Actually in standard G-code for filament extrusion, `E` is absolute; `G92 E0` sets current extruder position to 0; subsequent `G1 E..` are absolute amounts extruded. But here they set E-.7 then E.7 then segments with E .01926—that suggests after `G92 E0`, E values are *relative deltas* per move? No, in the same stream we see `G92 E0` near line 94. Then G1 E2, etc. Let's inspect context.

Actually in Prusa gcode, `G1 E-.7` is a retraction of 0.7mm, and subsequent segments use E values that are cumulative! But if E resets via `G92 E0` after prime, then the first segment `G1 X57.481 Y85.701 E.01926` would be E=0.01926 absolute from 0. But preceding line `G1 E.7 F1500` would set E=0.7, then next `G1 X... E.01926` would go backwards; firmware would see E decreasing and treat as retraction, not extrusion. Hmm.

Wait, maybe in this dialect, `G1 E.7` after a retraction is a "reactivate" command where E is relative (destretch) and subsequent E values are per-segment increments, not cumulative. Let's inspect more carefully. The first input segment after startup: 
```
G92 E0
M221 S100 
G21 
G90 
M83 
M572 S0.036 
M142 S36 
M107

G1 Z.2 F720
G1 E.7 F1500
M204 P500
G1 F2400
G1 X57.481 Y85.701 E.01926
```
It seems G92 E0 resets extruder. Then `G1 E-.7` retracts (E=-0.7). Then `G1 E.7` unretract? But if `G1 E.7` sets E back to 0.7, then subsequent `E.01926` would be less, which is weird. Unless the firmware interprets E as relative for each `G1`, i.e., delta? On Prusa firmware (based on Marlin?), I recall `G1 E` is absolute: you specify target absolute E position. But slicer-generated gcode uses absolute values. Here, after G92 E0, E target becomes 0.7, then moves to 0.01926 would retract—that can't be intended. So maybe there is a nonstandard "E" mode where E is incremental? Actually recent Prusa firmware has "M83" set extruder relative mode? `M83` is extruder relative mode in Marlin! In relative mode, `G1 E` values are incremental per move. Yes! `M83` selects relative extruder mode on Marlin. So `G1 E.01926` means extrude 0.01926 relative to current positionunció. That explains it. And `G92 E0`, `M83` relative mode? In relative mode G92 resets? Anyway, for our purpose, E value > 0 indicates extrusion move (relative), E negative retraction. So we should treat any `G1` with positive E plus X/Y as extrusion, independent of previous (because relative mode), while E=0 or no E as travel. Also statements like `G1 E.7` positive with no X/Y are prime/retract, not drawing.

So adjust parser: segment is extrusion if E token present and > 0 (and X/Y changed). Let's re-parse.
[tool-098] exec_command <- Re-extract extrusion segments using relative E positive ($ cat > /tmp/render_first_block.py <<'PY' import re from PIL import Image, ImageDraw current_obj = None last = None blocks = [] current_segs = None with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f, 1): line = line.strip() if not line: continue m = re.match(r'M486\s+S([-\d.]+)', line) if m: s = int(float(m.group(1))) if s == 0: current_obj = 0 current_segs = [] blocks.append((idx, current_segs)) elif s == 1: current_obj = 1 current_segs = None else: current_obj = None continue if current_obj == 0: if line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0'): tok = line.split() d = {} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t) > 1 and t[0] in 'XYZEFI': try: d[t[0]] = float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None and 'X' in last and 'Y' in last: x1,y1 = last['X'], last['Y'] x2,y2 = d['X'], d['Y'] # relative mode: positive E means extrusion if d.get('E', 0) > 0: current_segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) # update full position last = d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k] = d[k] for bi, (sline, segs) in enumerate(blocks): print("block", bi, "line", sline, "segs", len(segs)) if not segs: continue xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy = min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) res = 0.02 w = int((maxx-minx)/res)+2 h = int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print(" bbox", round(minx,2), round(maxx,2), round(miny,2), round(maxy,2), "img", w,h) img = Image.new('L',(w,h),255) dr = ImageDraw.Draw(img) for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: dr.line([(round((x1-minx)/res),round((y1-miny)/res)),(round((x2-minx)/res),round((y2-miny)/res))], fill=0, width=4) img.save(f'/tmp/block{bi}.png') print(" saved /tmp/block{bi}.png") PY python3 /tmp/render_first_block.py)

[tool-e2d] exec_command <- Render block2 segments to ASCII art ($ cat > /tmp/ascii_block.py <<'PY' import re def get_block(line_start): # returns segments for S0 block starting at line_start current_obj = None; last=None; segs=[] with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and re.match(r'M486\s+S',line.strip()): break if inblock and (line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0')): tok=line.split(); d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None and 'X' in last and 'Y' in last and d.get('E',0)>0: segs.append((last['X'],last['Y'],d['X'],d['Y'])) last=d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k]=d[k] return segs segs = get_block(1409) xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('segs',len(segs),'bbox',minx,maxx,miny,maxy) res=0.02 w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+1; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+1 grid=[[False]*w for _ in range(h)] def raster(x1,y1,x2,y2): px1=int((x1-minx)/res); py1=int((y1-miny)/res) px2=int((x2-minx)/res); py2=int((y2-miny)/res) dx=abs(px2-px1); dy=abs(py2-py1) sx=1 if px1<px2 else -1; sy=1 if py1<py2 else -1 err=dx-dy; x,y=px1,py1 while True: grid[y][x]=True if x==px2 and y==py2: break e2=2*err if e2>-dy: err-=dy; x+=sx if e2<dx: err+=dx; y+=sy for s in segs: raster(*s) # ascii, 2 cols per char import sys for oy in range(0,h,2): row=[] for ox in range(0,w,2): c=0 for yy in (oy,oy+1) if oy+1<h else (oy,): for xx in (ox,ox+1) if ox+1<w else (ox,): c+=grid[yy][xx] row.append('##' if c else ' ') print(''.join(row).rstrip()) PY python3 /tmp/ascii_block.py)

Actually, let's reconsider what "embossed text" means: In PrusaSlicer / PS "Emboss" feature, you can place text on a mesh surface ablating it. The generated G-code objects include "Shape-Box" (the base box or the face geometry) and "Embossed text" (the text geometry added on top / cut). When printing text onto an existing object, the text may be a separate object overlapping the base. The G-code orders: print Shape-Box (baseplate) first, then Embossed text over it.

[tool-464] exec_command <- Render big block 15014 and OCR ($ cat > /tmp/render_big.py <<'PY' import re from PIL import Image, ImageDraw line_start=15014 segs=[] last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and re.match(r'M486\s+S',line): break if inblock and (line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0')): tok=line.split(); d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None and 'X' in last and 'Y' in last and d.get('E',0)>0: segs.append((last['X'],last['Y'],d['X'],d['Y'])) last=d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k]=d[k] print('segs',len(segs)) xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('bbox',minx,maxx,miny,maxy) res=0.01 w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+2; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print('img',w,h) img=Image.new('L',(w,h),255) dr=ImageDraw.Draw(img) for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: dr.line([(int((x1-minx)/res),int((y1-miny)/res)),(int((x2-minx)/res),int((y2-miny)/res))],fill=0,width=2) img.save('/tmp/big.png') print('saved') PY python3 /tmp/render_big.py echo '--- OCR psm 6 ---' tesseract /tmp/big.png stdout --psm 6 2>/dev/null | head -40 echo '--- OCR psm 11 ---' tesseract /tmp/big.png stdout --psm 11 2>/dev/null | head -40)

[tool-0ed] exec_command <- Render big block as coarse ASCII overview ($ cat > /tmp/ascii_big.py <<'PY' import re line_start=15014 segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if inblock and (line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0')): tok=line.split(); d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: if last is not None and 'X' in last and 'Y' in last and d.get('E',0)>0: segs.append((last['X'],last['Y'],d['X'],d['Y'])) last=d.copy() elif last is not None: for k in d: last[k]=d[k] xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('segs',len(segs),'bbox',minx,maxx,miny,maxy) # grid overview e.g. 200 cols, 80 rows cols=200; rows=80 w=maxx-minx; h=maxy-miny grid=[[0]*cols for _ in range(rows)] for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: # mark cells touched: sample along line steps=max(1,int(max(abs(x2-x1),abs(y2-y1))/0.3)) for i in range(steps+1): t=i/steps x=x1+(x2-x1)*t; y=y1+(y2-y1)*t cx=int((x-minx)/w*cols); cy=int((y-miny)/h*rows) if 0<=cx<cols and 0<=cy<rows: grid[cy][cx]+=1 outs=[] for r in range(rows): row=''.join(' ' if grid[r][c]==0 else ('#' if grid[r][c]<3 else '@') for c in range(cols)) outs.append(row.rstrip()) print('\n'.join(outs)) PY python3 /tmp/ascii_big.py)

There are G2/G3 arc commands in the big block. Many G2 small arcs with I=-0.02 J=0.063—these might represent rounded corners of text strokes. The G3 commands with larger arcs (I=2.303, J=1.603) represent larger circular arcs.

So we must render arcs. Let's implement parsing G1/G2/G3 into segments (approximate arcs by polylines) for block 11 based on relative E>0 (extrusion) for G2/G3 too.

Important: The command lines have E values; G2/G3 also have E. Also, need to maintain current position through G0/G1/G2/G3 moves.

Let's write robust parser:
- Track current X,Y,E? For each line either G0/G1/G2/G3.
- Parse X,Y,I,J,E,F.
- For G1: if E>0 and has X,Y, segment line.
- For G2/G3: if E>0 and has X,Y,I,J, compute arc from current point to target with center at (curX+I, curY+J) (standard Marlin semantics: I and J are offsets from start to center). Need to know clockwise direction: G2 = clockwise? In Marlin, G2 = clockwise arc, G3 = counterclockwise. Actually CNC convention: G2 = clockwise, G3 = counterclockwise (from spindle perspective on XY plane). Marlin follows: G2 CW, G3 CCW? Let's verify with sample: G2 X57.992 Y86.411 I-.02 J.063, current X57.936 Y86.541? It's a small arc with center offset left+up. This creates rounded corner. Direction likely matches G2 CW. We'll render both: step along arc, sweep angle determined by direction. Compute start angle from center to current, end angle from center to target. For G2 (CW), sweep decreasing angle; for G3 (CCW), sweep increasing. Handle full circles and edge cases. If arc math not exact, approximate.

Then render.

Let's implement and re-render big block to PNG (we'll sample maybe only file region 15014-end). Then OCR and/or ASCII at multiple scales. Let's do it.
[tool-8a7] exec_command <- Rasterize big block including arcs ($ cat > /tmp/render_big_arcs.py <<'PY' import re, math from PIL import Image, ImageDraw line_start=15014 segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue if line[0] not in 'GG' : # quick # actually commands start with G0/G1/G2/G3 pass tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: last=d.copy() else: last=d.copy() continue ext = d.get('E', 0) > 0 hasxy = 'X' in d and 'Y' in d if hasxy and ext: x1,y1=last['X'],last['Y']; x2,y2=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=x1+d['I']; cy=y1+d['J'] r=math.hypot(x1-cx,y1-cy) a1=math.atan2(y1-cy,x1-cx); a2=math.atan2(y2-cy,x2-cx) # G2 clockwise (negative), G3 counterclockwise (positive) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(4,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.02)+2) px,py=x1,y1 for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)) px,py=nx,ny # update state (X/Y always overwritten if present; also Z/E etc) nx=last.copy() for k,d_ in d.items(): nx[k]=d_ if 'X' in nx and 'Y' in nx: last=nx else: # could still have X or Y only? Rare; just merge last=nx print('segs incl arcs',len(segs)) xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('bbox',minx,maxx,miny,maxy) res=0.02 w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+2; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print('img',w,h) img=Image.new('L',(w,h),255) dr=ImageDraw.Draw(img) pin=0.0 for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: dr.line([(int((x1-minx)/res),int((y1-miny)/res)),(int((x2-minx)/res),int((y2-miny)/res))],fill=0,width=2) img.save('/tmp/big_arcs.png') print('saved /tmp/big_arcs.png') PY python3 /tmp/render_big_arcs.py)

[tool-188] exec_command <- ASCII overview of big block with arcs ($ cat > /tmp/ascii_big_arcs.py <<'PY' import re, math line_start=15014 segs=[]; last=None def parse_bound(): global last inblock=False with open('text.gcode') as f: for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: if 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: last=d.copy() else: last=d.copy() continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: x1,y1=last['X'],last['Y']; x2,y2=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=x1+d['I']; cy=y1+d['J'] r=math.hypot(x1-cx,y1-cy) a1=math.atan2(y1-cy,x1-cx); a2=math.atan2(y2-cy,x2-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(4,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.05)+2) px,py=x1,y1 for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx parse_bound() print('segs',len(segs)) xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) cols=220; rows=110 w=maxx-minx; h=maxy-miny grid=[[0]*cols for _ in range(rows)] # use bounding segments; sample for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: steps=max(1,int(math.hypot(x2-x1,y2-y1)/0.2)) for i in range(steps+1): t=i/steps x=x1+(x2-x1)*t; y=y1+(y2-y1)*t cx=int((x-minx)/w*cols); cy=int((y-miny)/h*rows) if 0<=cx<cols and 0<=cy<rows: grid[cy][cx]+=1 for r in range(rows): print(''.join(' ' if grid[r][c]==0 else ('#' if grid[r][c]<3 else '@') for c in range(cols)).rstrip()) PY python3 /tmp/ascii_big_arcs.py)

Yeah the big block looks like a giant **S** shape (or perhaps a large **Z**?) made of thick strokes across the diagonal; there appear to be multiple bands. It looks like a single huge character occupying the entire plate, maybe "S". But also could be "Z" or "N"? Let's compare with ASCII of Z and S.

Actually looking at the coarse ASCII again, top-left has two small separate blobs at top, then a thick diagonal band sweeping down-right, and at bottom-right more blobs. That resembles a **large "S"** (upper curve → diagonal → lower curve). Hmm, but an S's diagonal goes down-left (from upper right to lower left). Here the diagonal goes down-right, which matches a **"Z"** if rotated 45°? A Z has top horizontal bar, diagonal down-left, bottom horizontal. Rotated -45° (clockwise), top bar becomes diagonal down-right; bottom bar becomes diagonal down-right too. Could be a lot of possibilities.

Wait, the overall shape might not be a single letter but **multiple letters arranged along a diagonal baseline**—like text written along a diagonal line ("a word" rotated 45°). The blobs at top-left could be the first letter, the diagonal band the middle letters, and bottom-right the last letters, all rotated. The ASCII overview, if interpreted as text rotated -45°, might spell something. Let's examine: The pattern shows a sequence of blobs descending diagonally from top-left to bottom-rightativos. That's like text rotated clockwise 45° (baseline going down-right). If we rotate the image -45° (counterclockwise), we might see readable text. Let's render a rotated version and OCR.

That's a compelling idea: The text may be printed on a diagonal path (along a line from top-left to bottom-right). The object "Embossed text" might be a text *on a curve/line*, positioned diagonally on the box. The G-code coordinates would then tilt the letters 45°. OCR on unrotated image fails because letters are rotated. The coarse ASCII of 45°-tilted text looks like diagonal bands—exactly.

Let's rotate the rendered image by -45° (or +45°?) and OCR. The baseline goes from top-left to bottom-right: that's rotation angle about +45° (clockwise). To make horizontal, rotate clockwise by 45°? In image coordinates (y down), a baseline from top-left to bottom-right has positive slope (dy/dx = +1), which corresponds to text rotated +45° (clockwise in standard math? Actually in image coords, slope +1 means angle +45°). To undo, rotate by -45° (counterclockwise) i.e., PIL rotate(-45) maybe. Let's test with PIL's rotate on the image. But the image is huge (7448x3360); rotation will expand. We can use PIL.Image.rotate(angle, expand=True, resample=NEAREST) and then OCR.

Let's create rotated versions (e.g., ±45°, maybe ±30° or ±60°). Use `expand=True`, and OCR. The toolpaths are only lines, so rotation should work.

Let's do it.

OCR still gibberish. Maybe the image is too dark/hatched so OCR can't isolate letters. Let's think differently: Maybe the big block is not text at all but a **grid/support** for the actual text model? Or it's the "Shape-Box" being printed under object0? Wait, line 15014 M486 S0 means object0 (Embossed text), so the paths from there onward are exclusively text object. The text object in the project might be a huge *single glyph* filling most of the plate (like "S" logo), printed after the box base. The user's actual text may be **a single character** and the question "what will the text show?" is a trick: it will show whatever single character is baked in, and we need to recognize it visually.

Let's inspect the model more: Maybe the "Embossed text" is actually a *single letter* (like "R") and the early small blocks are parts of that letter printed in earlier layers because the text is **slanted**. Wait, but line 15014's coordinate span is huge (55-204, 80-147). A single letter spanning 149mm width by 67mm height is plausible for a large text plate. And the ASCII looks like a single glyph filling the area. Its shape from ASCII: many horizontal/diagonal bands? Could be a huge stylized **"S"**. However, a font glyph (like "S") should have clear stroke shapes; our image looks like scribble because all extrusion segments include **both outline and infill** overlapping to form a dense silhouette. To recognize the glyph shape, we should render the **union of strokes as solid filled area**. The glyph's silhouette (filled) is what the printed text will look like (embossed text is solid letters). The silhouette is determined by the **outer boundaries/perimeters**; infill lines just fill inside the boundary.

If we render all toolpaths thickly, the silhouette may be filled, but OCR sees many specks. Let's create a **filled** representation: draw lines with thickness larger (e.g., width 8 at 0.02mm res = 0.16mm strokes). Since toolpath infill lines are spaced maybe ~0.4mm, width 0.16 might not fully fill gaps. We can thicken further and/or do morphological closure. Better: render at 0.05mm/pixel with width 4 (0.2mm), or use many passes.

Alternatively, we can **flood-fill outside** and keep inside as black to get solid silhouettes. Since text glyph shapes are closed outlines, flood-fill from background in a rendered binary image leaves interior white and exterior black (or vice versa). Invasively, we can use PIL/ImageDraw to draw closed polygons and flood fill. If perimeters are closed loops, the interior of each letter can be filled. Let's try a simpler approach: use a 2D array and `floodfill` from all border cells, then invert to get filled interiors. But if strokes aren't fully closed (due to arcs missing), edges may leak.

Let's implement with arcs + draw into a numpy threshold grid normal size (maybe 1500x700), thick lines, then flood fill outside using a deque. We'll need numpy; let's install numpy if not already (not installed by default? Let's check).

Now create a filled image. Approach:
1. Parse big block with arcs into segments.
2. Rasterize at res=0.05 mm/px into binary 0/1 array (wall=1).
3. Thicken by drawing with width maybe 1 and also dilate by 2 px.
4. Flood fill outside (starting from all border pixels) with 2; inner unvisited becomes solid (1).
5. Save PNG and OCR.

But if the toolpaths are not closed loops (e.g., single stroke text like handwritten/wireframe), flood fill won't work. Let's inspect the early small block shapes: They looked like outlineloops? The "P"-like shape had an outline, maybe a closed loop. The big block's silhouette might be closed too.

Let's implement flood-fill using numpy + BFS. Use scipy? Could install scipy but maybe not needed; implement BFS with deque on numpy array (could be big ~3000x1400 pixels = 4.2M cells, BFS okay). Or use PIL's floodfill: `ImageDraw.floodfill` can fill a connected region given a seed. That might be easier: Use PIL Image with black strokes on white; call `ImageDraw.floodfill(img, (x,y), value=0, thresh=...)` to fill regions? Actually floodfill replaces pixels similar to seed with new value. If we flood the white **background** from a corner with black, the interior of letters (surrounded by stroke lines) remain white; then invert. But background is big and connected; floodfill on whole image might be slow but PIL handles.

Let's write renderer: draw strokes on `L` image with white bg. Then flood fill from top-left (0,0) point with fill 128 (gray). Since background around all letters is connected, it turns gray; letters' interior (if closed) remains white. Then we can threshold: black = letters (white pixels that are inside). Or we can fill interior with black directly by floodfilling each white region not connected to border.

To compute interior regions not connected to border, do connected components via BFS over white pixels, mark those that touch border as "outside", and all others as "inside". Then output image where inside=black, outside=white. This works regardless of line thickness and fills text glyph interiors.

Risk: if perimeters are not closed (opening), interior leaks to border and won't fill alert. But we'll see.

Let's implement with numpy/BFS for big block.

But first, maybe simpler: render ASCII of the silhouette by **taking the thick union** and reducing resolution. Actually the coarse ASCII already reveals the overall silhouette—it looks like a **big "S"** or maybe some word. Let's try to read the ASCII more carefully. I'll zoom into specific regions (e.g., top, middle, bottom) with higher resolution ASCII to discern characters.

The text object might actually be a **single very large letter "S"**. Given the print is called "text.gcode" and the object is "Embossed text", maybe the user set the text to a single character "S"? The question "what will the text show?" would then have answer "S". But we need confidence.

Let's examine the rendered big block at higher ASCII resolution focusing on top-left region. The top-left `@@@` area could be the top curve of an "S". [tool-5be] exec_command <- Render filled silhouette of big block via flood fill ($ cat > /tmp/fill_big.py <<'PY' import re, math from PIL import Image, ImageDraw import numpy as np from collections import deque line_start=15014 segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: last=d.copy() if ('X' in d and 'Y' in d) else d.copy() continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: x1,y1=last['X'],last['Y']; x2,y2=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=x1+d['I']; cy=y1+d['J'] r=math.hypot(x1-cx,y1-cy) a1=math.atan2(y1-cy,x1-cx); a2=math.atan2(y2-cy,x2-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(4,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.01)+4) px,py=x1,y1 for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('segs',len(segs),'bbox',minx,maxx,miny,maxy) res=0.04 w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+2; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 print('img',w,h) img=Image.new('L',(w,h),0) # black background dr=ImageDraw.Draw(img) for x1,y1,x2,y2 in segs: dr.line([(int((x1-minx)/res),int((y1-miny)/res)),(int((x2-minx)/res),int((y2-miny)/res))],fill=255,width=2) arr=np.array(img) # 0 background, 255 strokes mask = arr > 50 # Connected region from border: BFS on non-mask visited = np.zeros((h,w), dtype=bool) dq=deque() for x in range(w): if not mask[0][x] and not visited[0][x]: visited[0][x]=1; dq.append((0,x)) if not mask[h-1][x] and not visited[h-1][x]: visited[h-1][x]=1; dq.append((h-1,x)) for y in range(h): if not mask[y][0] and not visited[y][0]: visited[y][0]=1; dq.append((y,0)) if not mask[y][w-1] and not visited[y][w-1]: visited[y][w-1]=1; dq.append((y,w-1)) while dq: y,x=dq.popleft() for dy,dx in ((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)): ny,nx=y+dy,x+dx if 0<=ny<h and 0<=nx<w and not visited[ny][nx] and not mask[ny][nx]: visited[ny][nx]=True; dq.append((ny,nx)) # Solid inside = not visited and not mask inside = (~visited) & (~mask) out = Image.new('L',(w,h),255) outarr = np.array(out) outarr[inside] = 0 outarr[mask] = 0 Image.fromarray(outarr).save('/tmp/big_filled.png') print('saved /tmp/big_filled.png') PY python3 /tmp/fill_big.py)

[penguin] output cap clamped hard: max_tokens 256000 -> 44351 (context_window 128000, estimated input 82625)
The filled silhouette at 180 cols looks like a big solid mass with a few holes at top-left. That doesn't reveal letters—it seems all one connected blob. Wait, maybe my fill is wrong because the text path is not closed (or our parser incorrectly connects all strokes forming one giant connected mass). It shows a huge solid region that fills almost the entire bounding box, with some cutouts at top-left (white gaps). That looks like a giant square-ish solid with holes—not text.

This suggests the "big block" object0 gcode does NOT trace glyph outlines; rather it might be a **perimeter/fill** of a large box shape (e.g., an "S" formed by printing a solid area? But the filled silhouette covers ~all area, meaning object0's extrusion paths span a huge rectangle almost the full plate, with a few holes in top-left). Actually the filled output implies the extruded paths create a **closed region covering almost the entire bounding box**. Because BFS from border failed to reach inside—everything not stroke is one connected interior. This might be because our parser accidentally connected separate strokes into one closed boundary via **travel moves**? No, we only use extrusion segmentshola. Or our arc parsing is wrong causing lines connecting weird places, closing the whole area.

Maybe we should NOT treat all extrusion as fill. The big block might actually be **the "Shape-Box"** itself, not text, because the M486 S0 at 15014 could indicate object0 is the big box? Wait, header: M486 AEmbossed text (object 0), M486 AShape-Box (object1). But maybe the association order is that object 0 is "Shape-Box"? Let's double-check: 
```
M486 S0
M486 AEmbossed text
M486 S-1
M486 S1
M486 AShape-Box
M486 S-1
```
M486 A attaches name to current selected object S. So S0 = "Embossed text", S1 = "Shape-Box". At line 15014 S0 => "Embossed text". Digest.

But the "Embossed text" object might be **the entire large box surface** (the text *is embossed into* the Shape-Box? Actually "Embossed text" could be a text object spanning big area). Hmm.

Let's look at the actual coordinates / widths of "text" letters in normal text prints. If the text object is a phrase like "HI" it would be a series of small closed shapes (letters), not one giant blob. Our filled view of block11 shows one giant blob with a couple of holes—suggesting the big block's paths, when filled, form **one large shape** (maybe a large "O" or "D" with holes), or something like **a large "S"** (solid S shape would have holes? No, S is a single closed stroke shape with interior connected; if flood-filled using stroke outline, the interior of the S (the space inside the stroke) would be black and background white; but the S curve is one closed shape—fills solid). Indeed a large solid "S" would appear as one connected blob spanning the bounding box. The ASCII filled output looks like a solid S? Hard to tell.

Let's visually inspect the filled ASCII at higher resolution. The earlier "all @@@@" makes it hard to see because PIL thumbnail turns solid black into @@@. Let's instead render binary ASCII (only # for black) at higher res without shading, e.g., 200x120, to see the shape/outline. PIL thumbnail with NEAREST merges to mixed shades; use threshold to get pure black/white then print `#`. Let's generate a monochrome ASCII of /tmp/big_filled.png at maybe 200x120.
[tool-e1d] exec_command <- Monochrome ASCII of filled silhouette ($ cat > /tmp/png_to_binascii.py <<'PY' from PIL import Image import sys src=sys.argv[1] cols=int(sys.argv[2]) if len(sys.argv)>2 else 180 rows=int(sys.argv[3]) if len(sys.argv)>3 else 120 img=Image.open(src).convert('L') img.thumbnail((cols,rows), Image.LANCZOS) w,h=img.size px=img.load() for y in range(h): line='' for x in range(w): v=px[x,y] line += '#' if v<128 else ' ' print(line.rstrip()) PY echo '===== monochrome filled 200x140 =====' python3 /tmp/png_to_binascii.py /tmp/big_filled.png 220 140 | head -150)
✓ [approved]

Let's implement a simple clustering: treat all segment endpoints as nodes; union segments whose endpoints within threshold (~0.3mm). Then count clusters and bbox. This could be expensive for 56k segs but manageable with spatial hashing.

Let's do it in Python with a grid hash, union via DSU. We'll parse big block with arcs and then build graph connect segments by endpoints within tolerance.
[tool-351] exec_command <- Cluster big block segments into connected components ($ cat > /tmp/cluster_big.py <<'PY' import re, math line_start=15014 segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: last=d.copy() if ('X' in d and 'Y' in d) else d.copy(); continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: x1,y1=last['X'],last['Y']; x2,y2=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=x1+d['I']; cy=y1+d['J'] r=math.hypot(x1-cx,y1-cy) a1=math.atan2(y1-cy,x1-cx); a2=math.atan2(y2-cy,x2-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(2,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.05)+2) px,py=x1,y1 for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx print('segs',len(segs)) # DSU on segment indices; endpoints hash grid # Use grid cell = 0.25 mm TOL=0.5 parent=list(range(len(segs))) def find(x): while parent[x]!=x: parent[x]=parent[parent[x]]; x=parent[x] return x def union(a,b): ra,rb=find(a),find(b) if ra!=rb: parent[rb]=ra # spatial grid over endpoints cell=0.3 grid={} def add_point(x,y,idx): key=(int(x/cell),int(y/cell)) grid.setdefault(key,[]).append(idx) for i,(x1,y1,x2,y2) in enumerate(segs): add_point(x1,y1,i); add_point(x2,y2,i) # Union segments sharing a grid cell (endpoints near) for (gx,gy), idxs in grid.items(): for a in range(len(idxs)): x1=segs[idxs[a]][0]; y1=segs[idxs[a]][1] for b in range(a+1,len(idxs)): # maybe check all endpoints distance; but cell small x2=segs[idxs[b]][0]; y2=segs[idxs[b]][1] if (x1-x2)**2+(y1-y2)**2 <= TOL*TOL: union(idxs[a],idxs[b]) x2=segs[idxs[b]][2]; y2=segs[idxs[b]][3] if (x1-x2)**2+(y1-y2)**2 <= TOL*TOL: union(idxs[a],idxs[b]) x1=segs[idxs[a]][2]; y1=segs[idxs[a]][3] x2=segs[idxs[b]][0]; y2=segs[idxs[b]][1] if (x1-x2)**2+(y1-y2)**2 <= TOL*TOL: union(idxs[a],idxs[b]) x2=segs[idxs[b]][2]; y2=segs[idxs[b]][3] if (x1-x2)**2+(y1-y2)**2 <= TOL*TOL: union(idxs[a],idxs[b]) # Count components comp_segs={} for i in range(len(segs)): r=find(i) comp_segs.setdefault(r,[]).append(segs[i]) print('num components',len(comp_segs)) # list components with seg count and bbox, sorted by x,y items=[] for r,ss in comp_segs.items(): xs=[v[0] for v in ss]+[v[2] for v in ss] ys=[v[1] for v in ss]+[v[3] for v in ss] items.append((len(ss), min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys), r)) items.sort(key=lambda t:(t[1],t[3])) for n,x0,x1,y0,y1,r in items: print(f"n={n:5d} x={x0:7.2f}-{x1:7.2f} y={y0:7.2f}-{y1:7.2f} w={x1-x0:.2f} h={y1-y0:.2f}") PY python3 /tmp/cluster_big.py)

[tool-9e2] exec_command <- Render and OCR major text components ($ cat > /tmp/render_components.py <<'PY' import re, math, os from PIL import Image, ImageDraw from collections import deque, defaultdict line_start=15014 segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: last=d.copy() if ('X' in d and 'Y' in d) else d.copy(); continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: x1,y1=last['X'],last['Y']; x2,y2=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((x1,y1,x2,y2)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=x1+d['I']; cy=y1+d['J'] r=math.hypot(x1-cx,y1-cy) a1=math.atan2(y1-cy,x1-cx); a2=math.atan2(y2-cy,x2-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(2,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.01)+2) px,py=x1,y1 for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx print('total segs',len(segs)) TOL=0.12 parent=list(range(len(segs))) def find(x): while parent[x]!=x: parent[x]=parent[parent[x]]; x=parent[x] return x def union(a,b): ra,rb=find(a),find(b) if ra!=rb: parent[rb]=ra cell=0.1 grid={} def add_point(x,y,idx): grid.setdefault((int(x/cell),int(y/cell)),[]).append(idx) for i,(x1,y1,x2,y2) in enumerate(segs): add_point(x1,y1,i); add_point(x2,y2,i) for idxs in grid.values(): for a in range(len(idxs)): ia=idxs[a]; x1=segs[ia][0]; y1=segs[ia][1] for b in range(a+1,len(idxs)): ib=idxs[b]; s=segs[ib] if (x1-s[0])**2+(y1-s[1])**2 <= TOL*TOL or (x1-s[2])**2+(y1-s[3])**2 <= TOL*TOL: union(ia,ib) x1=segs[ia][2]; y1=segs[ia][3] if (x1-s[0])**2+(y1-s[1])**2 <= TOL*TOL or (x1-s[2])**2+(y1-s[3])**2 <= TOL*TOL: union(ia,ib) comp_segs=defaultdict(list) for i in range(len(segs)): comp_segs[find(i)].append(segs[i]) items=[] for r,ss in comp_segs.items(): xs=[v[0] for v in ss]+[v[2] for v in ss] ys=[v[1] for v in ss]+[v[3] for v in ss] items.append((len(ss), min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys), r, ss)) items.sort(key=lambda t:(t[1],t[3])) os.makedirs('/tmp/comps',exist_ok=True) import subprocess for idx,(n,x0,x1,y0,y1,r,ss) in enumerate(items): if n < 200: continue print(f"COMP {idx}: segs={n} bbox=({x0:.2f},{y0:.2f})-({x1:.2f},{y1:.2f}) w={x1-x0:.2f} h={y1-y0:.2f}") # render res=0.02 w=int((x1-x0)/res)+2; h=int((y1-y0)/res)+2 img=Image.new('L',(w,h),0) dr=ImageDraw.Draw(img) for sx1,sy1,sx2,sy2 in ss: dr.line([(int((sx1-x0)/res),int((sy1-y0)/res)),(int((sx2-x0)/res),int((sy2-y0)/res))],fill=255,width=2) arr=bytearray(img.tobytes()) # flood fill BFS on black bg? We'll use PIL floodfill instead? Use numpy import numpy as np a=np.array(img) mask=a>50 hh,ww=mask.shape visited=np.zeros((hh,ww),bool) dq=deque() for x in range(ww): if not mask[0][x] and not visited[0][x]: visited[0][x]=1; dq.append((0,x)) if not mask[hh-1][x] and not visited[hh-1][x]: visited[hh-1][x]=1; dq.append((hh-1,x)) for y in range(hh): if not mask[y][0] and not visited[y][0]: visited[y][0]=1; dq.append((y,0)) if not mask[y][ww-1] and not visited[y][ww-1]: visited[y][ww-1]=1; dq.append((y,ww-1)) while dq: y,x=dq.popleft() for dy,dx in ((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)): ny,nx=y+dy,x+dx if 0<=ny<hh and 0<=nx<ww and not visited[ny][nx] and not mask[ny][nx]: visited[ny][nx]=1; dq.append((ny,nx)) inside=(~visited)&(~mask) outarr=np.full((hh,ww),255,dtype=np.uint8) outarr[inside]=0; outarr[mask]=0 Image.fromarray(outarr).save(f'/tmp/comps/c{idx}.png') # OCR multiple psm for psm in (6,11): try: r=subprocess.run(['tesseract',f'/tmp/comps/c{idx}.png','stdout','--psm',str(psm)],capture_output=True,text=True,timeout=60) txt=r.stdout.strip().replace('\n',' | ') print(f" psm{psm}: {txt}") except Exception as e: print(' ocr err',e) PY python3 /tmp/render_components.py)

In gcode, infill lines are typically long diagonal lines crossing the shape; outlines are short segments that follow the glyph contour. If we can separate out the **perimeters**, we'd see clear letters.

What's the difference in gcode? Slicer usually draws perimeters first, then infill, spiral vases etc. There's no TYPE comment in this gcode, but we can filter by **segment slope / length**. Infill lines are long straight diagonal lines (like the G-code lines "G1 X... E.4" with large distances) because they fill the interior; outlines/perimeters are curved, made of many short segments (with G1/G2 arcs). Font glyph outlines would be made of many short segments (both curved strokes).

Let's examine the "Shape-Box" object's boundaries: M555 X25.0793 Y2.99429 W199.855 H203.855 gives print area? The "Shape-Box" may be the **bed outline** and object "Embossed text" is the text shape printed on top of the box. The big block S0 may be the **text at its topmost layer** ("lid"). Its geometry we saw spans a diagonal swath across the plate—could be a **single word written diagonally** (like "HELLO" rotated 45°). If so, the ASCII overview with thick blobs corresponds to letters that are too large/tilted to read; we need to rotate properly and OCR at higher quality.

Let's take the **big block** image `/tmp/big_arcs.png` (7448x3360), crop/concentrate on the "text" region (non-white), rotate by appropriate angle, and try OCR again with maybe better parameters. Earlier rotated OCR gave gibberish because the image had lots of black infill/noise and the letters may be huge/too dense. Perhaps we should render **only outline paths**—maybe by using a **skeleton thinning** of the filled region, or by using the original arcs/G1 lines at higher quality but only short-curved segments (the outlines). Actually, the ASCII overview of all segments looked like a massive scribble; but the *filled* silhouette looked like solid shapes. OCR of filled comps gave shapes that seem not letters because flood fill merged across unclosed openings in the strokes.

Maybe the correct way to read font outlines is to use **PNG of the raw vector strokes** (all segments once) at very high resolution *without* infill (if we can separate perimeters). The gcode from slicer usually includes extrusion moves for **perimeters** and **infill**. For an embossed text on a curved surface, the slicer might generate only perimeters (no infill) because text is a thin shell. Then all segments ARE the outlines. Our raw render of big block already showed (in coarse ASCII) a diagonal scribble with several blobs—could be the actual word shape in script font. The filled silhouette looked like solid chunks because the toolpaths themselves are **solid strokes** (the letters are made of thick short strokes), so all segments together form a solid mass; flood fill doesn't add much.

Let's try to read the raw vector render using a more capable OCR (like EasyOCR) or by zooming in specific components. Let's install `pytesseract` already did. Tesseract fails on artistic/rotated fonts. Maybe install `easyocr`? It's heavy but doable (PyTorch not needed? EasyOCR uses torch; perhaps too big). Alternatively, use `opencv` with contour analysis? Better: Let's attempt to **view** the raw vector image as ASCII at high enough resolution to recognize letters—maybe the letters are **large enough** and the infill *is* the text mask.

Let's zoom into components. For comp0 (bbox 7.35x9.85 mm), render its raw (not filled) to ASCII at high res (e.g., 120x120) to see if it spells a letter. The filled ASCII for comp0 looked like a **vertical stroke with a counter/opening**—could be a "P" or "D"? Let's dump it with larger chars and 1:1 aspect.

continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: xa,ya=last['X'],last['Y']; xb,yb=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((xa,ya,xb,yb)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=xa+d['I']; cy=ya+d['J'] r=math.hypot(xa-cx,ya-cy) a1=math.atan2(ya-cy,xa-cx); a2=math.atan2(yb-cy,xb-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(2,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.005)+2) px,py=xa,ya for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,py if False else ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('bbox',minx,maxx,miny,maxy) res=0.01 w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+1; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+1 grid=[[0]*w for _ in range(h)] def rast(x1,y1,x2,y2): px1=int((x1-minx)/res); py1=int((y1-miny)/res) px2=int((x2-minx)/res); py2=int((y2-miny)/res) dx=abs(px2-px1); dy=abs(py2-py1) sx=1 if px1<px2 else -1; sy=1 if py1<py2 else -1 err=dx-dy; x,y=px1,py1 while True: grid[y][x]=1 if x==px2 and y==py2: break e2=2*err if e2>-dy: err-=dy; x+=sx if e2<dx: err+=dx; y+=sy for s in segs: rast(*s) # ASCII with 2x1 aspect for oy in range(0,h,2): row='' for ox in range(0,w,2): c=0; tot=0 for yy in (oy,oy+1) if oy+1<h else (oy,): for xx in (ox,ox+1) if ox+1<w else (ox,): c+=grid[yy][xx]; tot+=1 row += '#' if c else ' ' print(row.rstrip()) PY python3 /tmp/raw_ascii_comp.py)

Let's use cv2 to find contours in `/tmp/big_filled.png` and draw the **outermost contours** onto a new image)Skip, then OCR/ASCII. This should reveal the letter shapes (boundaries, no infill). Since flood fill created a solid shape, the contour should be the boundary of the text glyph(sdistinct).

[tool-71a] exec_command <- Extract outer contours from filled image ($ cat > /tmp/contours.py <<'PY' import cv2, numpy as np img = cv2.imread('/tmp/big_filled.png', cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE) # black shape on white: invert so shape is white _, bin = cv2.threshold(img, 127, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV) contours, hierarchy = cv2.findContours(bin, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE) print('num external contours', len(contours)) # sort by area cnt_area = [(cv2.contourArea(c), c) for c in contours] cnt_area.sort(reverse=True) out = np.full_like(img, 255) # draw all external contours (outer boundary) as black 2px for area,c in cnt_area[:20]: cv2.drawContours(out, [c], -1, 0, 4) x,y,w,h = cv2.boundingRect(c) print('area', int(area), 'bbox', x,y,w,h) cv2.imwrite('/tmp/big_contours.png', out) # Also draw each contour separately? maybe merged print('saved') PY python3 /tmp/contours.py echo '--- OCR contours ---' tesseract /tmp/big_contours.png stdout --psm 6 2>/dev/null | head -20 echo '--- ASCII contours ---' python3 /tmp/png_to_binascii.py /tmp/big_contours.png 200 120 | head -120)

The filled contour ASCII again shows what appears to be a **giant "S"**? Let's mentally visualize: Top left has a curve like the top of an S, then diagonal stroke downward rightwards, and bottom right a curve curling back left. It looks like an **"S" lying diagonally** or perhaps a **"Z"**. Hmm. The external contours list had 10 components; biggest one at bbox (169,59,1211,662) and another at (1860,754,1411,709). These two large contour blobs could be the top-left and bottom-right parts of a single "S"? Actually with RETR_EXTERNAL, the filled big image has 10 separate contours—the shape may be split due to flood-fill gaps.

OCR on contours gave "SR / NT"—maybe those are two parts? This is inconclusive.

Let's step back and think about the **source**: "text.gcode" for Prusa MK4s. This may come from a **sample file** commonly used to test text printing—often a file that prints **"P", "R", "U", "S", "A"**? The "Input shaper" line 37 suggests the printer has input shaping, not relevant.

Maybe the file is from Prusa's **emboss text tutorial** where the text is "Hello" or "123". The question "what will the text show?" may be a trick answer: **it won't show any text** because the gcode prints a priming line / wipe pattern whose shape looks like something (e.g., a "P" and "?").

Let's examine the small pre-15014 blocks again. They are printed **before** the main big block.youtube The first block (line 117) with 2 segs, then block line 1409 with 37 segs — these small shapes looked like **"P"** and **"?"**. Maybe these are **test/priming patterns** the printer prints at the start of each object to prime the nozzle—often called "prime line" shaped like a **letter "P" and "?"**? Actually, the printer may print a **priming "brim"** that scribbles a pair of characters, and the actual text is on the plate.

Wait—could the puzzle be that when running the print, the visible "text" is **not the intended text**, but something else like "P?" (prime pattern mistakes), or the *actual* text is embedded elsewhere?

Let's read the early blocks (line 117, 1409) as **the actual text**? The block at line 1409 drew a "P" and a "?" shape? Let's inspect those small shapes more carefully; they might *spell a short word* like "Pi" or "Po"? Let's render block1 (line 117, 2 segs) and block2 (line 1409) to see. block2 ASCII earlier showed a big "P"-ish shape and a small "?"-ish shape. Could the intended text be **"P?"** no.

Let's render block1 and block2 fully at high res + ASCII. We already saw block2's ASCII: the first glyph looked like "P" and second looked like "?" (or "a"?). Let's visualize block1 too.
[tool-3ed] exec_command <- Render block1 (line 117) ASCII ($ cat > /tmp/ascii_block_generic.py <<'PY' import re, math, sys line_start=int(sys.argv[1]) segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: last=d.copy() if ('X' in d and 'Y' in d) else d.copy(); continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: xa,ya=last['X'],last['Y']; xb,yb=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((xa,ya,xb,yb)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=xa+d['I']; cy=ya+d['J'] r=math.hypot(xa-cx,ya-cy) a1=math.atan2(ya-cy,xa-cx); a2=math.atan2(yb-cy,xb-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(2,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.01)+2) px,py=xa,ya for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,py if False else ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx if not segs: print('no segs'); sys.exit() xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) print('segs',len(segs),'bbox',round(minx,2),round(maxx,2),round(miny,2),round(maxy,2)) res=0.015 w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+2; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 grid=[[0]*w for _ in range(h)] def rast(x1,y1,x2,y2): px1=int((x1-minx)/res); py1=int((y1-miny)/res) px2=int((x2-minx)/res); py2=int((y2-miny)/res) dx=abs(px2-px1); dy=abs(py2-py1) sx=1 if px1<px2 else -1; sy=1 if py1<py2 else -1 err=dx-dy; x,y=px1,py1 while True: grid[y][x]=1 if x==px2 and y==py2: break e2=2*err if e2>-dy: err-=dy; x+=sx if e2<dx: err+=dx; y+=sy for s in segs: rast(*s) for oy in range(0,h,2): row='' for ox in range(0,w,2): c=0; tot=0 for yy in (oy,oy+1) if oy+1<h else (oy,): for xx in (ox,ox+1) if ox+1<w else (ox,): c+=grid[yy][xx]; tot+=1 row += '#' if c else ' ' print(row.rstrip()) PY echo '===== block line 117 =====' python3 /tmp/ascii_block_generic.py 117 echo '===== block line 1409 =====' python3 /tmp/ascii_block_generic.py 1409 | head -100)

[tool-c86] exec_command <- Render cropped ASCII for each early block ($ cat > /tmp/ascii_blocks_crop.py <<'PY' import re, math, sys def get_grid(line_start, res=0.015): segs=[]; last=None with open('text.gcode') as f: inblock=False for idx, line in enumerate(f,1): line=line.strip() if not line: continue if idx==line_start: inblock=True elif inblock and line.startswith('M486'): break if not inblock: continue tok=line.split(); cmd=tok[0] if cmd not in ('G0','G1','G2','G3'): continue d={} for t in tok[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZIJEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if last is None: last=d.copy() if ('X' in d and 'Y' in d) else d.copy(); continue ext=d.get('E',0)>0 if ext and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: xa,ya=last['X'],last['Y']; xb,yb=d['X'],d['Y'] if cmd in ('G0','G1'): segs.append((xa,ya,xb,yb)) elif cmd in ('G2','G3') and 'I' in d and 'J' in d: cx=xa+d['I']; cy=ya+d['J'] r=math.hypot(xa-cx,ya-cy) a1=math.atan2(ya-cy,xa-cx); a2=math.atan2(yb-cy,xb-cx) if cmd=='G2': if a2>=a1: a2-=2*math.pi else: if a2<=a1: a2+=2*math.pi steps=max(2,int(abs(a2-a1)*r/0.005)+2) px,py=xa,ya for i in range(1,steps+1): t=i/steps; a=a1+(a2-a1)*t nx=cx+r*math.cos(a); ny=cy+r*math.sin(a) segs.append((px,py,nx,ny)); px,py=nx,ny nx=last.copy() for k,v in d.items(): nx[k]=v last=nx if not segs: return None xs=[v[0] for v in segs]+[v[2] for v in segs] ys=[v[1] for v in segs]+[v[3] for v in segs] minx,maxx,miny,maxy=min(xs),max(xs),min(ys),max(ys) w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+2; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 grid=[[0]*w for _ in range(h)] def rast(x1,y1,x2,y2): px1=int((x1-minx)/res); py1=int((y1-miny)/res) px2=int((x2-minx)/res); py2=int((y2-miny)/res) dx=abs(px2-px1); dy=abs(py2-py1) sx=1 if px1<px2 else -1; sy=1 if py1<py2 else -1 err=dx-dy; x,y=px1,py1 while True: grid[y][x]=1 if x==px2 and y==py2: break e2=2*err if e2>-dy: err-=dy; x+=sx if e2<dx: err+=dx; y+=sy for s in segs: rast(*s) # crop rows=[r for r in range(h) if any(grid[r])] cols=[c for c in range(w) if any(grid[r][c] for r in range(h))] if not rows or not cols: return None y0,y1=rows[0],rows[-1]; x0,x1=cols[0],cols[-1] return grid,y0,y1,x0,x1 for line_start in [117, 1409, 2825, 4259, 4699, 5141, 5693, 7366, 8883, 10366]: out=get_grid(line_start) if out is None: print('----',line_start,'empty'); continue grid,y0,y1,x0,x1=out print(f'===== block line {line_start} =====') for y in range(y0,y1+1,2): row='' for x in range(x0,x1+1,2): # 2x2 block: mark if any filled c = any(grid[yy][xx] for yy in (y,y+1) if yy<len(grid) for xx in (x,x+1) if xx<len(grid[0])) row += '#' if c else ' ' print(row.rstrip()) PY python3 /tmp/ascii_blocks_crop.py)

The output is huge again because cropping failed—the grid is filled with leading blanks because the shape spans large? Wait, the crop function uses `cols=[c for c in range(w) if any(grid[r][c]...)]`, which should crop; but the output has tons of leading whitespace, meaning columns near x0 are blank? Maybe the issue: the shape occupies only a few pixels wide near x=0, and after cropping x0=0 so leading spaces remain from the shape's internal blank margins? Actually the entire output is dominated by huge blank columns between the two strokes (the two parts of "P" are far apart: the stem at left and bowl at right). So crop doesn't remove internal blank. That's okay. But the ASCII is huge because res=0.015mm and 2x1 scale, so each letter is ~140 chars tall.

We don't need to view ASCII at full res. Let's instead render PNG per early block and view via **image display**—but we can't see. However, we can **downscale** and print ASCII with smaller dims (e.g., 60 cols). Instead of full-res ASCII, use PIL thumbnail of the PNGs we already created in /tmp/blocks (b1..b9). Let's use png_to_binascii with small dims like 80x60. That will show the letter shapes clearly.

Actually block1 (line 1409) clearly "P"; block2 (line 2825) looks like maybe "R" (stem + two diagonal legs + bowl)? Hmm block2's ASCII shows a stem and perhaps a loop on the right and diagonal legs—could be "R"! Block3 (line4259) shows very sparse strokes—maybe "U"? Block4 (line4699) sparse — maybe "S"? Block5..9 progressively less decipherable, with shapes extending to the right (positions shift).

Wait, maybe these blocks are **cross-sections of a single letter "P" as it is sliced layer by layer**. The object "Embossed text" might be a single **"P"** printed with **slanted top surface**; each layer's S0 block prints a horizontal slice of the letter at increasing Z, and the XY positions shift because the letter is on a slope. The first block (line 1409) shows the full outline of "P" because the Z slice cuts through the whole letter? But block4-9 look like partial slices (only fraction of the letter), indicating the letter is being printed in slices.

Then the **big block at line 15014** prints the **top surface/lid** of the "P" (which is a full silhouette spanning the letter's footprint?) Wait, but big block spans a huge 150mm area, way larger than the "P" (7mm). Hmm.

Wait, maybe the early blocks are **one letter "P"** and later blocks are **different letters** as the print progresses along a diagonal text layout. But our block positions shift only slightly (from x57 to x66), not across the entire plate. The big block at 15014 spans all x55-204 — maybe it contains **all letters** of a long text, printed as the "top surface" layer.

Let's inspect the **Z values** of the S0 blocks: 
- 1409: Z? M486 S0 then Z.48 
- 2825: from block list, start Z maybe 1.08? Actually the previous S1 ended at some Z; the S0 then Z .?? Let’s check Z values at start of each S0 block.
From earlier block list:
- line 117: start last {'X':51,'Y':-4,'Z':0.8} -> then sets Z .2? so layer Z=0.2
- line 1409: last {'Z':0.8} then Z.48 -> Z layer?
- line 2825: after S1 block3 starting at Z? last from line 1529 {'Z':1.08} and then S0 at 2825 likely Z? Let me check: the blocks alternate: S0 line 1409 (Z .48), S1 line1529 (Z? maybe .76?), S0 line2825 (Z? maybe 1.04?), etc. So each S0 block is a **different layer** printing **part of the text**. 

This is the key: The text is being printed as a **3D solid** (embossed) **over many layers**, and each layer prints the cross-section of the text at that Z. The text is **tilted/slanted** (not flat), so the cross-section at each Z is a different slice of the letters. The final big block at line 15014 may print the **topmost layer** where the full text shape is apparent? Or the big block could be the **top surface** (cap) that spans the entire footprint of a **large text** (many letters).

Actually, the XY footprint of the text in early blocks is small (x≈57-66) and the big block at 15014 spans x55-204. This suggests the text object's footprint is **wide** (150mm) and the early small blocks are only the **first part / first letters** of the text visible in cross-section? But cross-sections of a word should show all letters at once (same bbox), not just one region. Unless the text is printed on a **diagonal/sloped plane** where different parts of the word are at different Z, so at a given Z only a band of the word (a small subset) is intersected. That aligns with coordinates shifting (each S0 block covers a slightly larger/different region as Z increases, representing the text on a slanted plane).

Given all this complexity, perhaps the correct answer is simpler and deliberately encoded.

Let's reconsider the puzzle from a high level. The file "text.gcode" is likely **auto-generated by the tester** for this exercise. The question: "When I run the print, what will the text show?" The answer might be found by *interpreting the toolpaths* as text. But maybe there is an easier built-in clue: The **object labels** are "Embossed text" and "Shape-Box". The actual text is "Embossed text" object content. In PrusaSlicer, when you select "Text" emboss, the generated filename and object names might be something like "Embossed text" but the underlying STL text is **"HELLO"** or similar. The Z values increment: 0.2, 0.48, 0.76, 1.04, 1.32, 1.6, 1.88, 2.16, 2.44, 2.72, 3.0, 3.32... Wait, the S0 blocks are at successive **layer heights** (each layer: Z .2/.48/.76/.../3.0). Wait, looking at the grep: line 10366 S0 then G1 Z2.72, and line 15014 S0 then Z3. So the early S0 blocks are layers Z=0.48,0.76,...3.0 (roughly every 0.28). And the big block (15014) is the **last layer** at Z=3.0 (the top of the text). So each block is a **different layer slice** of the embossed text.

So the "text" object has a **height** of about 3mm and at each layer a cross-section is printed; the cross-sections differ because the text is **3D geometry** (perhaps the word is **slanted/extruded along z**). The final layer (Z=3.0) is the **top surface**, which we saw as a giant "S"-like shape. The earlier layers are lower slices of the same 3D shape.

Actually, in 3D printing of a 3D model, each layer's slice is the intersection of the model with a horizontal plane. If the model is a **flat text** (extruded straight up), every layer's slice is the same shape (the same letters). But here the shapes change dramatically—this suggests the text is **not extruded straight up** but is **embossed/engraved onto a curved or slanted surface** (like the top of the Shape-Box), so the text's shape varies as it follows the surface.

The **top surface** (Z=3.0) shows the **full text** as it sits on the surface; our filled-contour visualization suggested a big "S". Now, to reconstruct the informative view, I should understand the geometry. The task: "When I run the print, what will the text show?" The user prints "Embossed text" onto a Shape-Box - this is the Prusa "text on object" feature where you engrave or emboss text onto a 3D model's surface® live inspection.

The key insight: the text is written onto a slanted face of the Shape-Box, so when viewed from above, the text is foreshortened/sheared. But the actual text itself will appear normally when viewed face-on to the surface.

To determine the actual text, I need to reconstruct the glyph coordinates in the face's local coordinate system. Let me figure out the surface plane.

Let me consider the "Shape-Box" - probably a box with a slanted top, like a nameplate. The text is placed on this slanted top face. In the G-code, printing happens layer by layer with Z increasing. But since the top face is slanted, the text is printed in an "embossed" way by printing extra material on top of the face, so the text's letters are printed along the slanted surface. The tool moves in 3D: X/Y/Z all vary.

Given the embossing, each letter is printed at a particular Z level along the surface. The earlier blocks (S0 blocks) each print at a fixed Z (0.2, 0.48, 0.76, ... 3.0) along diagonal - actually each "block" we identified by M486 S0 boundaries might not be one fixed Z; line 1409 starts Z=.48 but then has G1 with Z varying: "G1 X78.956 Y101.327 Z.943" etc. So the Z increases as we travel along the diagonal - one continuous slanted toolpath printing the whole "P" block? Actually those last G1 with Z ramp are travel moves (moving without extrusion) to reposition for the next segment of the text. The M486 S1 boundary after...

[tool-1bd] exec_command <- Render per-Z-level XY ext positions from big block ($ cat > /tmp/zbins.py <<'EOF' import re, math lines=open('/app/text.gcode').readlines() # collect segments with Z from state cur={} segs=[] # (z,x1,y1,x2,y2) last_xy=None for idx in range(15013, len(lines)): line=lines[idx].strip() if not (line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0')): continue d={} for t in line.split()[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'Z' in d: cur['Z']=d['Z'] if d.get('E',0)>0 and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: z=cur.get('Z',0) segs.append((z,d['X'],d['Y'])) # bbox xs=[s[1] for s in segs]; ys=[s[2] for s in segs] print('ext points',len(segs),'x',min(xs),max(xs),'y',min(ys),max(ys),'z',min(s[0] for s in segs),max(s[0] for s in segs)) # render each 10 mm Z bin res=0.15 for zb in range(0,80,10): pts=[(x,y) for z,x,y in segs if zb<=z<zb+10] if not pts: continue xs=[p[0] for p in pts]; ys=[p[1] for p in pts] minx,maxx=min(xs),max(xs); miny,maxy=min(ys),max(ys) w=int((maxx-minx)/res)+2; h=int((maxy-miny)/res)+2 grid=[[0]*w for _ in range(h)] for x,y in pts: gx=int((x-minx)/res); gy=int((y-miny)/res) if 0<=gx<w and 0<=gy<h: grid[gy][gx]=1 # ascii cropped, step 2 rows=[r for r in range(h) if any(grid[r])]; cols=[c for c in range(w) if any(grid[r][c] for r in range(h))] if not rows or not cols: continue print(f'=== Z bin {zb}-{zb+10} (pts={len(pts)}) bbox x {minx:.0f}-{maxx:.0f} y {miny:.0f}-{maxy:.0f} ===') for r in range(rows[0],rows[-1]+1,2): line='' for c in range(cols[0],cols[-1]+1,2): line += '#' if (grid[r][c] if r<len(grid) and c<len(grid[0]) else 0) or (r+1<len(grid) and c<len(grid[0]) and grid[r+1][c]) or (c+1<len(grid[0]) and grid[r][c+1]) else ' ' print(line.rstrip()) EOF python3 /tmp/zbins.py 2>&1 | head -220)

[tool-8e8] exec_command <- Fit plane Z(X,Y) and derive surface coords ($ cat > /tmp/plane.py <<'EOF' import re lines=open('/app/text.gcode').readlines() cur={}; pts=[] for idx in range(15013, len(lines)): line=lines[idx].strip() if not (line.startswith('G1') or line.startswith('G0')): continue d={} for t in line.split()[1:]: if len(t)>1 and t[0] in 'XYZEF': try: d[t[0]]=float(t[1:]) except: pass if 'Z' in d: cur['Z']=d['Z'] if d.get('E',0)>0 and 'X' in d and 'Y' in d: pts.append((d['X'],d['Y'],cur.get('Z',0))) print('points',len(pts)) # Fit z = a*x + b*y + c import numpy as np A=np.array([[x,y,1] for x,y,z in pts]); b=np.array([z for x,y,z in pts]) sol,resid,rank,sv=np.linalg.lstsq(A,b,rcond=None) a,b,c=sol print('plane z = %.6f*x + %.6f*y + %.6f' % (a,b,c)) print('resid mean',np.mean(np.abs(A@sol-b))) # gradient direction on xy-plane is (a,b) gx,gy=a,b glen=np.hypot(gx,gy) ux,uy=gx/glen,gy/glen vx,vy=-uy,ux print('grad dir (ux,uy)=',ux,uy,'perp (vx,vy)=',vx,vy) # project points onto plane coords u,v (distance along surface) # for an orthographic projection onto plane: local coords # u = (x-xo)*ux + (y-yo)*uy ; v = (x-xo)*vx + (y-yo)*vy xo,yo=60,80 us=[(x-xo)*ux+(y-yo)*uy for x,y,z in pts] vs=[(x-xo)*vx+(y-yo)*vy for x,y,z in pts] print('u range',min(us),max(us),'v range',min(vs),max(vs)) # Also estimate text size in v (since v is horizontal across surface, undistorted) EOF python3 /tmp/plane.py)
